#### C.3.3 Inaccurate Thought from Human Perspective

Personal knowledge limitations of human:

- •
  Source. The array of problems addressed by the human mind constitutes
  merely a specific subset within the spectrum of challenges tackled by
  AI systems [Griffiths \[2020\]](#bib.bib23), resulting from human time
  limitation and human computation limitation, etc.

  Due to the limited lifespan, whatever a human learns must be acquired
  in less than a million hours of real-time experience. The AlphaGo
  system that beat the best human players in history has the benefit of
  multiple human lifetimes of simulated play [Silver et al.
  \[2016\]](#bib.bib70).

  There exists a disparity between human intelligence and machine
  intelligence in addressing the Rubik’s Cube conundrum. Machine
  intelligence has successfully determined the shortest path from any
  initial configuration to an unscrambled state, requiring no more than
  20 moves [Rokicki et al. \[2014\]](#bib.bib63). Conversely, the most
  adept human solvers necessitate approximately 50 moves to achieve the
  same outcome.

- •
  Examples. Please refer to
  Tab. [17](#A3.T17 "Table 17 ‣ C.3.3 Inaccurate Thought from Human Perspective ‣ C.3 Real-world Examples ‣ Appendix C Benchmark ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")
  for examples.

|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Example 1                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| Question: What should be the next move in the game of Go?                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| Rationale: In order to win this game, I need to capture more of the opponent’s pieces. Since capturing as many of the opponent’s pieces as possible is advantageous, it is now time to launch an offensive and encircle their pieces.                                                                                                                    |
| Answer: Therefore, the next move is at position A.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| Explanation: In the context of playing Go, a deficiency in holistic board assessment and the incorporation of offensive strategies leads to inaccurate thought. This narrow focus solely on offensive tactics detrimentally impacts the overall outcome of the game, hindering the player’s ability to achieve victory across the entirety of the board. |
| Example 2                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| Question: How long will it take to solve the Rubik’s Cube at the fastest?                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| Rationale: One rotates a Rubik’s Cube in 0.2 second, only needing to know how many steps it takes to solve one. Someone solve a Rubik’s Cube requires a minimum of 50 steps. It takes 4 seconds to solve the Rubik’s Cube at the fastest.                                                                                                                |
| Answer: Therefore, the answer is 4 seconds.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| Explanation: In assessing the completion time of the Rubik’s Cube conundrum, due to a lack of knowledge regarding shortest path algorithms and planning, an individual relies solely on experiential intuition, presuming that a minimum of 50 steps is necessary, whereas in reality, the puzzle can be completed within 20 steps or fewer.             |

Table 17: Examples of human knowledge limitation.

Cognitive biases of human:

- •
  Source. [Tversky and Kahneman \[1974\]](#bib.bib80) define human
  cognitive biases: systematic patterns of deviation from rational
  judgment. Here we list three types of cognitive biases of human.

  Confirmation bias is a common cognitive bias, referring to the
  tendency of people to seek, remember, or emphasize information that
  supports their existing views or beliefs, while ignoring or
  disregarding information that contradicts their
  viewpoints [Del Vicario et al. \[2017\]](#bib.bib14).

  When members within a group suppress their individual capacity for
  independent thinking in order to maintain team harmony or consensus,
  groupthink occurs [Janis \[2008\]](#bib.bib31).

  Emotional reasoning happens when people judge the authenticity or
  value of things based on their own emotional state [Norris
  \[2000\]](#bib.bib56).

- •
  Examples. Please refer to
  Tab. [18](#A3.T18 "Table 18 ‣ C.3.3 Inaccurate Thought from Human Perspective ‣ C.3 Real-world Examples ‣ Appendix C Benchmark ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")
  for examples.

|                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Example 1                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| Question: Alice likes going to the coffee shop. Does Alice like drinking tea?                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Rationale: Alice likes going to the coffee shop. People who enjoy going to coffee shops must enjoy drinking coffee. However, individuals who enjoy drinking coffee may not necessarily enjoy drinking tea. Therefore, it cannot be inferred whether Alice enjoys drinking tea.                                |
| Answer: Therefore, not sure if Alice likes drinking tea.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| Explanation: Not all individuals who enjoy frequenting coffee shops necessarily enjoy drinking coffee. There is no inherent correlation between enjoying coffee shop visits and liking coffee. Here, the introduction of confirmation bias plays a pivotal role.                                              |
| Example 2                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| Question: Which one is the middle-aged woman? (A) The beautifully dressed slender woman, (B) The dowdily dressed slightly overweight woman,(C)Not sure.                                                                                                                                                       |
| Rationale: This woman is middle-aged. She needs to take care of her family and children, and she has no time to dress up or manage her figure. She could be either overweight or slim, inclined towards dressing up or adopting a more casual appearance; hence, we cannot ascertain her physical appearance. |
| Answer: Therefore, the answer is (C).                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Explanation: In the above example, groupthink leads to the maximization of societal and individual family interests, hence the collective belief that a middle-aged woman should be overweight and dowdily dressed. However, the reality is that middle-aged women are diverse and cannot be stereotyped.     |

Table 18: Examples of cognitive bias.

### C.4 Noise Generation

As detailed in
Sec. [3](#S3 "3 The NoRa Dataset ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
we have introduced both irrelevant and inaccurate noises into our
dataset as insertions. These insertional noises are carefully integrated
into the rationales, ensuring they neither modify the existing reasoning
pathways nor affect the final answers. Furthermore, they are
purposefully crafted based on the intermediate steps of the reasoning
sequence, which guarantees that each piece of noise is contextually
related to the stage of reasoning it accompanies. To maintain
consistency, we treat each sentence in the reasoning sequence as a
single thought; accordingly, each noise we introduce is also fashioned
as only one sentence. For irrelevant noise, we draw from unrelated
scientific or social facts. Alternatively, inaccurate noise involves the
extension of an intermediate thought, which is redundant and incorrect.

NoRa-Math. In NoRa-Math problems, an intermediate reasoning result
typically manifests as a numerical value. For instance, from the
equation "We have 4 + 2 = 6 in base-10. ", we take the derived number
$`6`$ and craft a sentence of noise to follow this particular reasoning
step. Take the numeral "6"; we might introduce an unrelated fact such as
"According to the Standard Model of physics, there are six types of
quarks, the fundamental constituents of matter." For inaccurate
thoughts, we generate a decimal addition related to $`6`$, such as "6 +
5 = 11. ", which is inaccurate in base-9 representation.

NoRa-Symbolic. NoRa-Symbolic problems are handled similarly; here,
intermediate results are individual elements of a navigation
instruction. We insert a sentence of noise that aligns with these
specific components. For example, if the element is the directive
"right," we fabricate a direction-related noise. One irrelevant thought
can be "Turning right in countries that drive on the right side of the
road typically does not intersect with oncoming traffic.". For
inaccurate thoughts, to ensure that the noise does not contradict the
previous reasoning, we select another related instruction on the same
term domain. For example, when explaining the instruction "right," we
might generate noise related to the instruction "left", such as "left
means I_TURN_RIGHT.", which is incorrect and does not contradict the
previous thought, and this thought itself is redundant.

NoRa-Commonsense. When it comes to NoRa-Commonsense problems, we
introduce noise that mirrors the relationships discerned during the
reasoning process. For example, after establishing that "mother’s sister
is aunt," we craft a noise sentence associated with the concept of
"aunt." An irrelevant noise insertion could be: "Aunts often play
pivotal roles in the social development of primates, akin to their
influence in human societies." To generate inaccurate thoughts, we might
produce a statement like "an aunt’s mother is also an aunt," which is
logically inaccurate.

In addition to inserting a fixed number of noisy thoughts per chain of
thought shot, as detailed in the main text, we further introduce
variability in noise addition by randomly inserting noise following each
thought. This randomness follows a Bernoulli distribution
$`\text{Bern}(\epsilon)\in\{0,1\}`$, where noise is added after a
thought only if the Bernoulli trial results in 1. Consequently, while
the fixed number approach guarantees a set amount of noise within a CoT
shot, the random addition allows for the possibility of varying amounts
of noise in each shot, dictated by the probability parameter
$`\epsilon`$.

### C.5 NoRa Datasheet

Following the framework of [Gebru et al. \[2021\]](#bib.bib20), we
provide a comprehensive datasheet for the NoRa dataset in
Tab. [19](#A3.T19 "Table 19 ‣ C.5 NoRa Datasheet ‣ Appendix C Benchmark ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").
This datasheet documents the motivation, composition, collection
process, preprocessing details, intended uses, distribution plan, and
maintenance strategy of our dataset.

|                                                                                                                                                    |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Motivation                                                                                                                                         |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| For what purpose was the dataset created?                                                                                                          | The NoRa dataset was created to evaluate the robustness of large language models when faced with noisy rationales in chain-of-thought prompting. It addresses the under-explored challenge of LLMs’ reasoning ability when presented with in-context examples containing irrelevant or inaccurate steps.                                    |
| Who created the dataset and on behalf of which entity?                                                                                             | The authors of this paper created the dataset.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| Who funded the creation of the dataset?                                                                                                            | Unavailable.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| Any other comment?                                                                                                                                 | The NoRa dataset is the first of its kind specifically designed to evaluate the robustness of LLMs against noisy rationales. It covers a wide range of reasoning tasks, including mathematical, symbolic, and commonsense reasoning, The dataset includes varying levels of noise in rationales to systematically access LLMs’ performance. |
| Composition                                                                                                                                        |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| What do the instances that comprise the dataset represent?                                                                                         | Each instance in the NoRa dataset consists of a question, its correct answer, and in-context demonstrations containing noisy reasoning steps.                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| How many instances are there in total?                                                                                                             | The NoRa dataset contains 26,391 questions in total (see Section 3.2). Table 2 contains the sizes of our datasets.                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| Does the dataset contain all possible instances or is it a sample (not necessarily random) of instances from a larger set?                         | The NoRa dataset is a carefully curated sample selected from a larger pool of potential reasoning tasks.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| What data does each instance consist of?                                                                                                           | Each instance in the NoRa dataset contains a question, its correct answer, and in-context demonstrations with noisy reasoning steps.                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| Is there a label or target associated with each instance?                                                                                          | Yes, each instance has a correct answer that serves as its label.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| Is any information missing from individual instances?                                                                                              | No, each instance contains complete information.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| Are relationships between individual instances made explicit?                                                                                      | Yes, instances are grouped by reasoning type: mathematical, symbolic, and commonsense.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| Are there recommended data splits?                                                                                                                 | No, this dataset is designed for training-free in-context learning. We provide recommended in-context demonstrations for each question.                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| Are there any errors, sources of noise, or redundancies in the dataset?                                                                            | No, the dataset itself is clean and does not contain any errors, unintended noise, or redundancies.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Is the dataset self-contained, or does it link to or otherwise rely on external resources?                                                         | Yes, the dataset is self-contained and does not rely on any external resources.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| Does the dataset contain data that might be considered confidential?                                                                               | No.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Does the dataset contain data that, if viewed directly, might be offensive, insulting, threatening, or might otherwise cause anxiety?              | No, the dataset does not include any offensive, insulting, threatening, or anxiety-inducing content.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| Collection                                                                                                                                         |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| How was the data associated with each instance acquired?                                                                                           | The data was created based on the reference of three rational datasets. (see sec 3.2)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| What mechanisms or procedures were used to collect the data?                                                                                       | We systematically added irrelevant or inaccurate thoughts to clean rationales from existing datasets. The irrelevant or inaccurate thoughts are generated from GPT4.                                                                                                                                                                        |
| If the dataset is a sample from a larger set, what was the sampling strategy?                                                                      | Whenever we use subsets, we randomly sample from the original data.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |

| Over what timeframe was the data collected?                                                                                                        | NoRa was created in 2023. The original datasets of NoRa (Base Calculation, SCAN, and CLUTRR) were created between 2018-2023. We added noisy rationales to these existing datasets to create NoRa.                                                                                                                                           |

Evaluating and improving LLMs’ robustness to noisy information 2. Developing new denoising techniques for reasoning tasks 3. Understanding the impact of In-context noise on LLMs.                                                                                                                   |
| Is there anything about the composition of the dataset or the way it was collected and preprocessed/cleaned/labeled that might impact future uses? | The dataset contains artificially introduced noise, which might not perfectly reflect real-world scenarios of noisy reasoning.                                                                                                                                                                                                              |

## Appendix D Theoretical Analysis

In this part, we provide the theoretical results of ICL with the noisy
demonstrations based on the distinguishability \[[Xie et al.,
2021](#bib.bib90)\], which acts as a crucial condition in understanding
ICL as the implicit Bayesian inference. We reveal that the noisy
demonstrations can enlarge the 0-1 risk of the in-context predictor
through the mismatch between the noisy prompt distribution and the clean
pre-trained distributions.

First, we briefly review some definitions and preliminary setups, as
well as the distinguishability condition established in \[[Xie et al.,
2021](#bib.bib90)\], which is used to formalize when in-context learning
occurs.

##### Clean and noisy demonstrations.

As stated previously, the ICL techniques prompt the LLM with a few
supporting examples $`S_{n}\!=\!\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}`$ collected
in the current context, each composed of a question $`x_{i}`$ and answer
$`y_{i}`$, and then constructing the new input of $`x_{\text{ICL}}`$ as

|     |                                                                                |                                                                                              |     |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\vskip-2.0ptx_{\text{ICL}}=\left[S_{n},x_{\text{test}}\right]`$ | $`\displaystyle=\left[x_{1},y_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{n},\;x_{\text{test}}\right].\vskip-2.0pt`$ |     |

With $`S_{n}`$, the
$`f_{\theta}(x_{\text{ICL}})\!\mapsto\!y_{\text{test}}`$ is easier than
$`f_{\theta}(x_{\text{test}})\!\mapsto\!y_{\text{test}}`$. Then, the CoT
further refines $`x_{\text{ICL}}`$ by constructing the step-by-step
rationale $`\mathcal{T}_{i}`$, consisting several thoughts
$`T_{i}^{(j)}`$, i.e.,

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |     |
| --- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\vskip-2.0ptx_{\text{CoT}}=\left[x_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1},y_{1},\ldots,x_{n},\mathcal{T}_{n},y_{n},\;x_{\text{test}}\right],\quad\text{where \;}\mathcal{T}_{i}=\left[T_{i}^{(1)},T_{i}^{(2)},T_{i}^{(3)},\ldots,T_{i}^{(k)}\right].\vskip-2.0pt`$ |     |

However, as mentioned, the thoughts in CoT
(Eqn. [2](#S3.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ 3.1 Definition of Noisy Rationales ‣ 3 The NoRa Dataset ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"))
can be noisy in practice. This noise can be attributed to (1) irrelevant
thoughts, which are irrelevant but correct, or (2) inaccurate thoughts,
which are relevant but factually wrong. Here, we uniformly formalize
these two kinds of noise as

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                       |     |     |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\vskip-2.0pt{\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{i}}=\left[T_{i}^{(1)},{\hat{T}_{i}^{(1)}},T_{i}^{(2)},{\hat{T}_{i}^{(2)}},\ldots,T_{i}^{(k)},{\hat{T}_{i}^{(k)}}\right],\vskip-2.0pt`$ |     | (4) |

where $`{\hat{T}_{i}^{(j)}}`$ represents a noisy thought (irrelevant or
inaccurate) that is coherent with previous clean thought $`T_{i}^{(j)}`$
(relevant and correct) in
Eqn. [D](#A4.Ex2 "Clean and noisy demonstrations. ‣ Appendix D Theoretical Analysis ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
and it make the $`x_{\text{CoT}}`$ be noisy.

##### In-context learning setups.

Keeping the same setups in \[[Xie et al., 2021](#bib.bib90)\], we
analyze the latent concept $`\theta`$ from a family of concepts
$`\Theta`$ that defines a distribution over the observed tokens $`o`$
from a vocabulary $`\mathcal{O}`$. Following \[[Xie et al.,
2021](#bib.bib90)\], we assume that the pertaining document is a length
$`T`$ sequence can be generated with the prior $`p(\theta)`$ as
$`p(o_{1},\cdots,o_{T})=\int_{\theta\in\Theta}p(o_{1},\cdots,o_{T}|\theta)p(\theta)d\theta`$,
where $`p(o_{1},\cdots,o_{T}|\theta)`$ is defined by the hidden markov
model. We have the prompt distribution $`p_{prompt}`$ that generates the
prompts for in-context learning, in which all the demonstrations share
the prompt concept $`\theta^{*}`$.

We build the derivation by analyzing the in-context predictor
$`f_{n}(x_{\text{test}})=\arg\max_{y}p(y|S_{n},x_{\text{test}})`$, which
indicates the most likely prediction over the pertaining distribution
conditioned on the prompt from the prompt distribution, assuming that
greedy decoding is adopted in practice. Specifically, we study the
expected 0-1 error with $`n`$ examples
$`L_{\text{0-1}}(f_{n})=\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}[f_{n}(x_{\text{CoT}}\neq y_{\text{test}})]]`$.

Given the output distribution
$`p_{\theta}^{i}(o):=p(\mathcal{O}[i]=o|\mathcal{O}[1:i-1],\theta)`$ of
the $`i`$-th token with previous tokens and the analogous distribution
$`p^{i}_{prompt}:=p_{prompt}(\mathcal{O}[i]=o|\mathcal{O}[1:i-1])`$
under the prompt distribution, the distinguishability condition is
dependent on a KL divergence between the previous two distributions and
the error terms $`\epsilon_{\theta}`$ resulting from the distribution
mismatch between the prompt and the pertaining distributions for each
example. Letting $`p_{\theta}^{i}(o)`$ and $`p^{i}_{prompt}`$ correspond
to the concepts $`\theta`$ and $`\theta^{*}`$, the KL divergence are
defined as follows,

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
\text{KL}_{i}(\theta^{*}||\theta):=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{O}[1:i-1]\sim p_{prompt}}[\text{KL}(p^{i}_{prompt}||p_{\theta}^{i})].
``` |  | (5) |

Then, the distinguishability is provided as,

###### Condition D.1 (distinguishability \[[Xie et al., 2021](#bib.bib90)\]).

$`\theta^{*}`$ is distinguishable if for all $`\theta\in\Theta`$,
$`\theta\neq\theta^{*}`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\text{KL}_{i}(\theta^{*}||\theta)>\epsilon_{\theta},`$ |  | (6) |

where $`\epsilon_{\theta}`$ is a small error term corresponding to the
latent concept $`\theta`$. It has been empirically verified \[[Wei
et al., 2023](#bib.bib86), [Wei et al., 2022b](#bib.bib85), [Xi et al.,
2023](#bib.bib89)\] that the larger example lengths $`k`$ can increase
the left-hand side and improve distinguishability. Intuitively, the
larger example lengths increase the proportion of the prompt sampled
from the pre-trained distribution, providing more evidence for Bayesian
inference. According to the noisy demonstration introduced in
Eqn. ([3](#S3.E3 "Equation 3 ‣ 3.1 Definition of Noisy Rationales ‣ 3 The NoRa Dataset ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")),
we can naturally have the following condition on the distinguishability
under the noisy scenario.

###### Condition D.2 (noisy distinguishability).

With the irrelevant or inaccurate noise injection, the distribution
mismatch between the prompt and the pre-trained distributions is also
enlarged, resulting in a varied requirement with error term
$`\xi_{\theta}(\delta)`$ (where $`\delta`$ indicates the noise ratio of
the noisy rational in the whole demonstration) for $`\theta^{*}`$ being
distinguishable if for all $`\theta\in\Theta`$,
$`\theta\neq\theta^{*}`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\text{KL}_{i}(\theta^{*}||\theta)>\epsilon_{\theta}+\xi_{\theta}(\delta),\quad{\rm where}\quad\xi_{\theta}(\delta)\propto\delta.`$ |  | (7) |

Given the above
Condition [D.2](#A4.EGx8 "Condition D.2 (noisy distinguishability). ‣ In-context learning setups. ‣ Appendix D Theoretical Analysis ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
we can analyze the expected 0-1 error when it fails according to the
noisy scenarios considered in our work. It indicates that ICL cannot
induce the correct output with the noise demonstration. First, we
provide the following lemma based on the results of [DeVore and Lorentz
\[1993\]](#bib.bib15) and [Xie et al. \[2021\]](#bib.bib90), which
relate the bound on KL divergence with 0-1 loss given the
Condition [D.2](#A4.EGx8 "Condition D.2 (noisy distinguishability). ‣ In-context learning setups. ‣ Appendix D Theoretical Analysis ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").

###### Lemma D.3 (noisy-relaxed bound in \[[Xie et al., 2021](#bib.bib90)\]).

let $`\mathcal{B}`$ denotes the set of $`\theta`$ which does not satisfy
Condition [D.2](#A4.EGx8 "Condition D.2 (noisy distinguishability). ‣ In-context learning setups. ‣ Appendix D Theoretical Analysis ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").
We assume that
$`\text{KL}(p_{prompt}(y_{\text{test}}|x_{\text{test}}))||p(y_{\text{test}}|x_{\text{test}},\theta)`$
is bounded for all $`\theta`$ and $`\theta^{*}`$ minimizes the
multi-class logistic risk as,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\begin{split}L_{\text{CE}}(\theta)=-\mathbb{E}_{x_{\text{test}}\sim p_{prompt}}[p_{prompt}(y_{\text{test}}|x_{\text{test}})\cdot\log p(y_{\text{test}}|x_{\text{test}},\theta)].\end{split}`$ |  |  | (8) |

We can have if

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x_{\text{test}}\sim p_{prompt}}[\text{KL}(p_{prompt}(y_{\text{test}}|x_{\text{test}},\delta)||p(y_{\text{test}}|x_{\text{test}},\theta))]\leq\epsilon_{\theta}+\xi_{\theta}(\delta),\quad\forall\quad\theta\in\mathcal{B},`$ |  | (9) |

then

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}L_{0-1}(f_{n})\leq\inf_{f}L_{0-1}(f)+g^{-1}\bigg(\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal{B}}(\epsilon_{\theta}+\xi_{\theta}(\delta))\bigg),`$ |  | (10) |

where
$`g(\tau)=\frac{1}{2}\big((1-\tau)\log(1-\tau)+(1+\tau)\log(1+\tau)\big)`$
is the calibration function for the multiclass logistic loss for
$`\tau\in[0,1]`$.

Based on the previous work \[[Kleijn and van der Vaart,
2012](#bib.bib37)\], we assume that there is continuity between the
concept parameter $`\theta`$ and its corresponding output distribution
$`p_{\theta}`$, where the KL divergence is assumed to have the 2nd-order
Taylor expansion. Then, we have the following theorem and proof.

###### Theorem D.4.

Given the previous assumption on multi-class logistic risk, we can have
that $`k\geq 2`$ and $`n\rightarrow\infty`$, the risk of an in-context
learning predictor $`f_{n}`$ is bounded as

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}L_{0-1}(f_{n})\leq\inf_{f}L_{0-1}(f)+g^{-1}\bigg(O(\frac{\gamma_{\theta}\cdot\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}(\epsilon_{\theta}+\xi_{\theta}(\delta))}{k-1})\bigg),\end{split}`$ |  |  | (11) |

where
$`g(\tau)=\frac{1}{2}\big((1-\tau)\log(1-\tau)+(1+\tau)\log(1+\tau)\big)`$
is the calibration function for multi-class logistic loss for
$`\tau\in[0,1)`$. The overall result indicates that the upper bound of
the risk is loosed by $`\xi_{\theta}(\delta)`$ of the noisy
demonstration.

###### Proof of Theorem [D.4](#A4.Thmtheorem4 "Theorem D.4. ‣ In-context learning setups. ‣ Appendix D Theoretical Analysis ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").

Assume KL divergence have a 2nd-order Taylor expansion around
$`\theta^{*}`$ as,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\forall i>1,\text{KL}_{i}(\theta^{*}||\theta)=\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\mathrm{T}}I_{i,\theta^{*}}(\theta-\theta^{*})+O(||\theta-\theta^{*}||^{3}),`$ |  | (12) |

where $`I_{i,\theta^{*}}`$ is the Fisher information matrix of the
$`i`$-th token distribution w.r.t. $`\theta^{*}`$. Let
$`\gamma_{\theta^{*}}=\frac{\max_{i}\lambda_{\text{max}}(I_{i,\theta^{*}})}{\min_{i}\lambda_{\text{min}}(I_{i,\theta^{*}})}`$
and $`\lambda_{\text{max}},\lambda_{\text{min}}`$ is the largest and
smallest eigenvalues. We can have the following relationship for
$`\theta\in\mathcal{B}`$ that,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{k}\text{KL}_{i}(\theta^{*}||\theta)`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\mathrm{T}}I_{i,\theta^{*}}(\theta-\theta^{*})+O(||\theta-\theta^{*}||^{3})`$ |  | (13) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}(k-1)\lambda_{\text{min}}(I_{i,\theta^{*}})||\theta-\theta^{*}||^{2},`$ |  | (14) |
|  | $`\displaystyle||\theta-\theta^{*}||^{2}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon_{\theta}+\xi_{\theta}(\delta)}{\frac{1}{2}(k-1)(\min_{i}\lambda_{\text{min}}(I_{i,\theta^{*}}))}.`$ |  | (15) |

Then we can bound the last KL term as,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\text{KL}_{k}(\theta^{*}||\theta)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\theta^{*})^{\mathrm{T}}I_{i,\theta^{*}}(\theta-\theta^{*})+O(||\theta-\theta^{*}||^{3})`$ |  | (16) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}(\max_{i}\lambda_{\text{max}}(I_{i,\theta^{*}})||\theta-\theta^{*}||^{2}+O||\theta-\theta||^{2})`$ |  | (17) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\frac{((\epsilon_{\theta}+\xi_{\theta}(\delta))\max_{i}\lambda_{\text{max}}(I_{i,\theta^{*}})+O(1))}{(k-1)\min_{i}\lambda_{\text{min}}(I_{i,\theta^{*}})}.`$ |  | (18) |

Denoting $`\text{KL}_{k}(\theta^{*}||\theta)`$ with
$`\mathbb{E}_{x_{\text{test}}\sim p_{prompt}}[\text{KL}(p_{prompt}(y_{\text{test}}|x_{\text{test}}(\delta))||p(y_{\text{test}}|x_{\text{test}},\theta))]`$,
we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x_{\text{test}}\sim p_{prompt}}[\text{KL}(p_{prompt}(y_{\text{test}}|x_{\text{test}}(\delta))||p(y_{\text{test}}|x_{\text{test}},\theta))]\leq\frac{((\epsilon_{\theta}+\xi_{\theta}(\delta))\max_{i}\lambda_{\text{max}}(I_{i,\theta^{*}})+O(1))}{(k-1)\min_{i}\lambda_{\text{min}}(I_{i,\theta^{*}})}.`$ |  | (19) |

Then, with the previous
Lemma [D.3](#A4.Thmtheorem3 "Lemma D.3 (noisy-relaxed bound in [ , ]). ‣ In-context learning setups. ‣ Appendix D Theoretical Analysis ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
we can have the theorem result: for $`k\geq 2`$ and
$`n\rightarrow\infty`$, the risk of an ICL predictor $`f_{n}`$ is
bounded as,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}L_{0-1}(f_{n})\leq\inf_{f}L_{0-1}(f)+g^{-1}\bigg(O(\frac{\gamma_{\theta}\cdot\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}(\epsilon_{\theta}+\xi_{\theta}(\delta))}{k-1})\bigg),\end{split}`$ |  |  | (20) |

where
$`g(\tau)=\frac{1}{2}\big((1-\tau)\log(1-\tau)+(1+\tau)\log(1+\tau)\big)`$
is the calibration function for multi-class logistic loss for
$`\tau\in[0,1)`$, and $`g^{-1}`$ is roughly linear in $`\epsilon`$ as
analyzed in \[[Xie et al., 2021](#bib.bib90)\].

∎

With the above results based on \[[Xie et al., 2021](#bib.bib90)\], we
can find that when the demonstration length is fixed, the multi-class
logistic loss can have a loosened upper bound controlled by our noise
injection. Intuitively, when the noise rate is large, the performance of
ICL can be significantly affected, which can be empirically verified by
the Easy-to-Hard performance trend evaluated in
Tab. [3](#S3.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 3.2 Tasks and Statistics ‣ 3 The NoRa Dataset ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").
On the other hand, considering the fixed noisy injection in the
demonstration, our method can reduce the effect of the mismatching error
by rephrasing the demonstration with the pre-trained distribution of the
LLM itself and then decreasing the error term in RHS of
Eqn. ([20](#A4.E20 "Equation 20 ‣ Proof of Theorem . ‣ In-context learning setups. ‣ Appendix D Theoretical Analysis ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")),
which increase the distinguishability of ICL to induce the correct
outputs successfully.

## Appendix E Implementation Details

In this section, we provide a comprehensive overview of the
implementation details for our experiments, including the specifics of
the baseline methods
(Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1 "E.1 Baseline Methods ‣ Appendix E Implementation Details ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")),
the full algorithm of our proposed CD-CoT method
(Appendix [E.2](#A5.SS2 "E.2 Contrastive Denoising with Noisy Chain-of-Thought ‣ Appendix E Implementation Details ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")),
and the hyper-parameter optimization process
(Appendix [E.3](#A5.SS3 "E.3 Hyper-parameter Optimization ‣ Appendix E Implementation Details ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")).
For reproduction, the source code and full dialogs are provided at:
[https://github.com/tmlr-group/NoisyRationales](https://github.com/tmlr-group/NoisyRationales).

To conduct the full range of experiments on GPT-3.5-turbo-0613 mentioned
in this paper, we utilized a total of 2.03B tokens. This consisted of
1.21B input tokens and 0.82B output tokens.

### E.1 Baseline Methods

In this part, we introduce the implementation details of the baseline
methods.

Self-correction Methods:

- •
  Intrinsic Self-correction (ISC) [Huang et al. \[2024\]](#bib.bib29)
  asks LLM to endeavor to rectify its initial responses based solely on
  its inherent capabilities. We employ the prompts from [Huang et al.
  \[2024\]](#bib.bib29), instructing LLMs to review and revise their
  answers to NoRa tasks with "Review your previous answer and find
  problems with your answer. Based on the problems you found, improve
  your answer. Please reiterate your answer."
- •
  Self-polish (SP) [Xi et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib89) teaches LLM to
  eliminate noisy information, rearrange the logic structure, and
  organize local conditions into new ones in parallel. We implement this
  method by (1) prompting LLMs to individually refine each noisy CoT
  examplar without additional information, repeating the process three
  times, and (2) combining these rephrased demos to form the context for
  the task.

Self-consistency Methods:

- •
  SmoothLLM (SM) [Robey et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib62) enhances robustness
  by injecting perturbations into the prompts and utilizing
  self-consistency to mitigate these effects. We apply the described
  disturbance methods to noisy rationale demonstrations and feed them
  into LLMs for reasoning tasks. This process is repeated five times,
  with the most common answer across iterations selected as the voted
  answer.
- •
  Self-denoise (SD) [Zhang et al. \[2023c\]](#bib.bib102) improves LLM
  robustness by preprocessing prompts with random masks; the LLMs then
  work to reconstruct the masked content, reducing noise and aiding
  incoherent reasoning. Our implementation involves (1) applying the
  masking method to the noisy rationales on each shot, (2) prompting the
  LLMs to infer and fill the mask sections of each demonstration,
  and (3) using the reconstructed CoT demonstrations for task reasoning.
  This process is also repeated five times, and the most common answer
  is selected.
- •
  Self-consistency (SC) [Wang et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib83) boosts
  reasoning performance by sampling multiple outputs and conducting
  majority voting without engaging in any input processing. To apply
  this method, we run the same task 5 times and vote for the maximum
  number of the same answers.

External Supervision Methods:

- •
  Self-correction with Oracle Feedback (SCO) [Huang et al.
  \[2024\]](#bib.bib29) enhances the ISC framework by providing a
  definitive ground truth. To implement this method, we use ISC to guide
  LLMs in refining their reasoning outcomes. The correction process is
  iterative, allowing for up to two adjustments, but ceases as soon as
  the correct answer is obtained.
- •
  Backtracking (BT) \[[Tyen et al., 2023](#bib.bib81)\] is a method of
  providing the position where the first mistake occurs to prompt LLMs
  to correct mistakes. To implement this method, (1) we prompt LLMs to
  correct the noisy rationales in each CoT example by providing the
  first noise sentence in it, and (2) we combine these rephrased
  examples to form the in-context for the task reasoning.
- •
  ContrastiveCoT (CC) \[[Chia et al., 2023](#bib.bib9)\] uses
  contrastive CoT demonstration to improve LLMs’ generalization
  abilities. We implement this method by providing an extra example with
  clean rationales as a positive demonstration, and the original prompts
  with noisy rationales are treated as negative examples. We then prompt
  the task queries with these positive and negative examples using the
  contrastive structure prompt mentioned in the paper to guide the LLMs
  in the reasoning process.

### E.2 Contrastive Denoising with Noisy Chain-of-Thought

Step-1: Rephrasing via Supervised Contrasting ($`1`$to$`N`$). First, we
establish a general prompt to construct a pair of contrastive examples,
as shown in
Sec. [5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 Implementation ‣ 5 Method ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
steering the model towards learning from the clean example and then
rephrasing as well as rectifying the noisy examples. It is worth noting
that for each dataset, the clean demonstration utilized is fixed, aiming
to minimize the usage of additional supervision. In the $`K`$-shot
in-context learning, we formulate a rephrasing prompt for each noisy
example in the context, following the template in
Sec. [5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 Implementation ‣ 5 Method ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").
The rephrasing time of each noisy example is controlled by the parameter
$`N`$. In our implementation, we set $`N=5`$ to strike a balance between
the diversity of rephrased results and computational costs.

Step-2: Rationale Selection ($`N`$to$`M`$, $`N\!\geq\!M`$). Given that
rephrased results may still contain noise, we employ answer matching for
further denoising. Specifically, for each noisy example and its
corresponding rephrased rationales, as each rationale can deduce an
answer to the question, we select the rationales that the corresponding
answers match the given (true) answer of this demonstration. This is
called the “answer matching” and does not require an LLM for inference.
The selected rationales form the refined candidate pool for that noisy
example.

For example, $`(x,\mathcal{T},y)`$ indicates the question, rationale,
and answer of a given noisy demonstration. The rationale rephrasing
(step 1) obtains three rephrased demonstrations:
$`(x_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},\mathcal{T}_{2},y_{2})`$, and
$`(x_{3},\mathcal{T}_{3},y_{3})`$. Then, if $`y_{1}=y_{2}=y`$ and
$`y_{3}\neq y`$, we will select the first two rephrased rationales,
$`\mathcal{T}_{1}`$ and $`\mathcal{T}_{2}`$. Namely, only rephrased
results with consistent answers are retained, forming the refined
candidate pool for that noisy demonstration of in-context learning.
Notably, in cases where no consistent answer is found, the algorithm
randomly selects one of the rephrased results as the candidate pool for
the noisy example. Subsequently, we assemble $`M`$ contexts using the
$`K`$ candidate pools. In particular, we randomly select one candidate
from each pool and concatenate the $`K`$ selected candidates, forming a
context. This process is repeated until $`M`$ contexts are obtained.

Step-3: Rationale Exploration ($`M`$to$`D`$, $`M\!\leq\!D`$). For each
of the $`M`$ distinct contexts, we perform multiple reasoning to explore
various rationales, resulting in a total number of reasoning times equal
to $`D`$. To maintain a fair comparison with other baseline methods and
to introduce more randomness in generating diverse reasoning paths, we
employ the default temperature parameter, i.e., 1. Notably, given the
fixed values for $`M`$ and $`D`$, distinct allocation strategies of
reasoning times can be generated, leading to performance and cost
differences, as illustrated in
Tab. [12](#S5.T12 "Table 12 ‣ 5.2 Empirical Study ‣ 5 Method ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")
and
Tab. [12](#S5.T12 "Table 12 ‣ 5.2 Empirical Study ‣ 5 Method ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").

Step-4: Answer Voting ($`D`$to1). Given $`D`$ answers from step 3, we
equally vote them into a final answer. For example, if the answer set is
$`{1,1,1,2,3}`$ where $`D=5`$, the answer voting will select “1” as the
final answer for its highest frequency.

### E.3 Hyper-parameter Optimization

We would like to further clarify the selection of hyper-parameters. To
ensure a fair comparison with other baselines (for example, in
self-consistency where we have the LLM reason 5 times and then vote), we
fixed both $`D`$ and $`N`$ to be 5. Specifically, $`D`$ represents
reasoning 5 times over the rephrased results, and $`N`$ indicates
performing contrastive rephrasing on the input sample 5 times. This
allows us to study the effects of reasoning with different rephrased
inputs.

$`M`$ represents the number of selected rephrased results from the
generated ones, therefore $`M`$ must be less than or equal to $`N`$, and
the value of $`M`$ depends on the breakdown combination of $`D`$.

When $`D=5`$, the possible combinations are
$`[(1,1,1,1,1),(1,1,1,2),(1,2,2),(3,2),(5)]`$, with corresponding $`M`$s
of $`[5,4,3,2,1]`$. For example, $`(1,1,1,1,1)`$ means inputting 5
different rephrased results and reasoning once for each, totaling 5
times of reasoning. It’s worth mentioning that the greater the value of
$`M`$, the larger the computational cost.

Finally, to balance the effectiveness and the cost of the algorithm, we
test the results on a subset of the dataset with $`M=1,2,3,5`$. We find
that the accuracy exhibits subtle variations when employing different
algorithm instances, with the highest average accuracy observed at 51.3%
and the lowest average accuracy at 49.3%. However, the difference in
computational cost is relatively significant. Therefore, we ultimately
select the algorithm instance with $`M=2`$ as the default setting and
test it across the entire dataset.

We present the impact of parameters $`M,D,`$ and $`C`$ on token usage in
Tab. [12](#S5.T12 "Table 12 ‣ 5.2 Empirical Study ‣ 5 Method ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").
Here, we conduct additional experiments to figure out the effect of
varying $`N`$ on the computational cost. Specifically, we maintain a
constant number of reasoning repetitions $`D=5`$ while adjusting $`N`$
and other parameters. Here are the configurations for testing:

1.  1.
    N=1, M=1, C=\[5\], D=5
2.  2.
    N=2, M=2, C=\[3,2\], D=5
3.  3.
    N=3, M=2, C=\[3,2\], D=5
4.  4.
    N=4, M=2, C=\[3,2\], D=5
5.  5.
    N=5, M=2, C=\[3,2\], D=5 (default)

These experiments are conducted on the NoRa-Math ase-9 task with
irrelevant hard noise. The
Tab. [30](#A5.T30 "Table 30 ‣ E.3 Hyper-parameter Optimization ‣ Appendix E Implementation Details ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")
shows the total number of tokens consumed by CD-CoT for complete
reasoning on 300 test samples. This includes tokens used for both
rephrasing and reasoning steps.

|        |         |         |         |         |         |
|--------|---------|---------|---------|---------|---------|
|        | $`N=1`$ | $`N=2`$ | $`N=3`$ | $`N=4`$ | $`N=5`$ |
| tokens | 1071560 | 1408845 | 1532606 | 1656617 | 1780095 |

Table 30: Ablation study of parameter $`N`$.

As we can observe, the number of tokens generated increases as $`N`$
increases. This growth in token count directly correlates with
computational needs. However, it’s important to note that the increase
is not linear, suggesting that the computational cost does not scale
linear proportionally with $`N`$.

## Appendix F Full Experiments

In this section, we provide additional experimental details and analyses
to supplement the main results presented in the paper. We conduct
experiments on various LLMs and reasoning tasks to comprehensively
evaluate the effectiveness of our proposed Contrastive Denoising
Chain-of-Thought (CD-CoT) method in handling noisy rationales.
Specifically,

- •
  In Appendix
  [F.1](#A6.SS1 "F.1 Detailed Setups of the Experiments ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
  we describe the detailed experimental setups, including the base
  language models used, evaluation settings, and CoT settings.
- •
  In Appendix
  [F.2](#A6.SS2 "F.2 Supplementary Results of the Main Experiments ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
  we present additional experimental results on different language
  models, and computational costs, and introduce the Normalized
  Difference in Accuracy (NDA) metric for evaluating the efficacy of
  methods under noisy scenarios.
- •
  In Appendix
  [F.3](#A6.SS3 "F.3 The Superior Performance and Denoising Effectiveness ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
  we conduct an ablation study to further justify the denoising power of
  the proposed CD-CoT method and highlight the importance of its two key
  components: contrastive denoising with rationale rephrasing and
  repeated reasoning with voting.
- •
  In Appendix
  [F.4](#A6.SS4 "F.4 The Difficulty of Irrelevant Noise Semantics ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
  we explore the impact of different semantic difficulty levels of
  irrelevant noise on model performance and the effectiveness of CD-CoT
  under these scenarios.
- •
  In Appendices
  [F.5](#A6.SS5 "F.5 The Number of Noisy Thoughts ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")
  and
  [F.6](#A6.SS6 "F.6 The Number of Noisy Examples ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
  we investigate how the number of noisy thoughts per example and the
  number of noisy examples affect the model’s reasoning performance.
- •
  In Appendix
  [F.7](#A6.SS7 "F.7 Variants of CD-CoT ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
  we discuss the variants of CD-CoT, including a self-supervised variant
  that does not rely on manually crafted clean examples.
- •
  In Appendix
  [F.8](#A6.SS8 "F.8 New Datasets and Large-scale Real Problems ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
  we further explore CD-CoT’s robustness by introducing new datasets.
  Moreover, we investigate the noisy rationale problem in large-scale
  real-world scenarios by evaluating the impact of noisy context on
  model performance in multi-turn conversational QA tasks using the
  MT-Bench [Zheng et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib108) dataset.
- •
  In Appendix
  [F.9](#A6.SS9 "F.9 Rephrased Examples of Different Denoising Methods ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
  we provide additional qualitative results, showcasing the denoised
  examples generated by different robust methods across various tasks
  under medium-level noise.

These additional experiments and analyses aim to provide a comprehensive
understanding of the noisy rationale problem, the effectiveness of the
proposed CD-CoT method, and the factors influencing model performance
under noisy scenarios.

### F.1 Detailed Setups of the Experiments

We employ GPT-3.5-turbo-0613 \[[Floridi and Chiriatti,
2020](#bib.bib17)\] as our base LLM (denoted as Base) for the analyses
presented in this study. In addition, we conduct evaluations on three
supplementary models, including Gemini-Pro (Jan. 2024) \[[Team et al.,
2023](#bib.bib76)\], Llama2-70B \[[Touvron et al., 2023](#bib.bib79)\],
and Mixtral-8x7B \[[Jiang et al., 2024](#bib.bib33)\]. While evaluating
baseline methods on various, we consistently keep the temperature
parameter $`\tau`$ and the top-p setting at their default value of 1,
along with all other hyperparameters of models set to defaults. We
conducted experiments on the first 300 questions for each task and
repeated reasoning 5 times for each question.

We assume all CoT experiments with clean rationales or noisy rationales
are conducted in a 3-shot setting unless specified otherwise.
Furthermore, all CoT examples are constructed by randomly drawing from
all available questions, except for the NoRa-Symbolic Longer task, which
has predefined demonstrating and testing scopes.

The Normalized Difference in Accuracy (NDA) Metric. We propose a new
evaluation score, Normalized Difference in Accuracy (NDA), to quantify
the efficacy of $`\mathcal{M}`$ under the noisy scenario,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\operatorname{NDA}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P})\!=\!\frac{\operatorname{Acc}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P}_{\text{noisy}})\!-\!\operatorname{Acc}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Q},\emptyset)}{\operatorname{Acc}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P}_{\text{clean}})\!-\!\operatorname{Acc}(\mathcal{M};,\mathcal{Q},\emptyset)},
``` |  | (21) |

where
$`\operatorname{Acc}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P}_{\text{clean}})`$,
$`\operatorname{Acc}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P}_{\text{noisy}})`$
and $`\operatorname{Acc}(\mathcal{M};,\mathcal{Q},\emptyset)`$ represent
the accuracy of method $`\mathcal{M}`$ with clean rationales, noisy
rationales (irrelevant or inaccurate), and without CoT demos.
Fig. [9](#A6.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ F.2 Supplementary Results of the Main Experiments ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")
is the illustration of the NDA metric.

Please note that: (1) The main evaluation metric used throughout this
paper is the accuracy score as introduced in Section 4; (2) The NDA
metric serves as an auxiliary tool for analyzing empirical results,
specifically designed to quantify how effectively a given LLM and
denoising method perform under noisy scenarios.

Tab. [32](#A6.T32 "Table 32 ‣ F.2 Supplementary Results of the Main Experiments ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")
presents a comparison of the accuracy and NDA across all methods. A
negative value in NDA indicates that the accuracy of noisy rationales
falls below that of 0-shot. We observe that CD-CoT consistently excels
in NDA, nearing 100% in most tasks and even surpassing it in certain
instances.

### F.3 The Superior Performance and Denoising Effectiveness

The proposed CD-CoT method denoises noisy rationales, which leads to
better reasoning performance. To be specific:

- •
  The first two steps of CD-CoT are for explicit data denoising. First,
  it rephrases the noisy example by contrasting it with the clean
  example. Then, with the obtained rephrased examples, it selects
  qualified candidates by checking the validity of the rephrased
  answers.
- •
  The rephrased (denoise) rationales by different robust methods are
  shown in
  Tab. [12](#S5.T12 "Table 12 ‣ 5.2 Empirical Study ‣ 5 Method ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").
  As can be seen, CD-CoT significantly removes noise and also ensures
  format alignment with the original rationale. More examples of
  denoising can be found in
  Appendix. [F.9](#A6.SS9 "F.9 Rephrased Examples of Different Denoising Methods ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").
  Hence, these empirical results adequately justify the denoising power
  of CD-CoT.

The two important aspects of the proposed method are (1) contrastive
denoising with rationale rephrasing (+ CD Rephrase) and (2) repeated
reasoning with voting (+ Vote). Here, we conduct an extra ablation study
on the NoRa-Math Base-9 dataset to justify the denoising power of
CD-CoT.

In this ablation study, we compare the performance of CD-CoT with
different combinations of clean and noisy rationales on the NoRa-Math
Base-9 dataset. The "Base model" column represents the accuracy of the
base model without any additional components. The "+ CD Rephrase" column
shows the accuracy when the contrastive denoising with rationale
rephrasing (CD Rephrase) is applied. The "+ Vote" column represents the
accuracy when the final answer is determined by a majority vote of five
runs. Finally, the "CD-CoT" column shows the accuracy of the complete
CD-CoT method, which includes both CD rephrasing and voting.

Since CD-CoT requires 3-shot noisy rationales and 1-shot clean rationale
for contrastive rephrasing, we compare its performance with the
following setups. The results are summarized in the
Tab. [34](#A6.T34 "Table 34 ‣ F.3 The Superior Performance and Denoising Effectiveness ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").

- •
  3-shot clean rationales (without CD-CoT)
- •
  3-shot noisy rationales (irrelevant or inaccurate) of varying
  difficulty levels + 1-shot clean rationale (with CD-CoT)
- •
  4-shot clean rationales (without CD-CoT)

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
| CoT shots | Base model | \+ CD Rephrase | \+ Vote | CD-CoT |
| 3-shot clean shots | 46.4 | — | 62.3 | — |
| 3-shot irrelevant-easy + 1-shot clean | 46.0 | 47.2 | 57.3 | 60.0 |
| 3-shot irrelevant-medium + 1-shot clean | 41.3 | 47.2 | 54.7 | 60.7 |
| 3-shot irrelevant-hard + 1-shot clean | 36.2 | 46.5 | 47.3 | 58.3 |
| 3-shot inaccurate-easy + 1-shot clean | 36.5 | 42.6 | 44.7 | 53.0 |
| 3-shot inaccurate-medium + 1-shot clean | 23.4 | 43.0 | 32.3 | 58.7 |
| 3-shot inaccurate-hard + 1-shot clean | 16.1 | 37.0 | 24.3 | 47.0 |
| 4-shot clean rationale | 50.8 | — | 67.3 | — |

Table 34: Ablation study of CD rephrase and vote components on the
NoRa-Math Base-9 dataset.

From the experimental results, we can observe that:

###### Observation F.1 (CD-CoT significantly improves reasoning performance across various noisy scenarios).

CD-CoT brings a significant improvement compared to the base model when
prompted with 3 noisy rationales + 1 clean rationale across various
noise ratios of both irrelevant and inaccurate noise. The base accuracy
of CD-CoT can be attributed to the improvement brought by the
contrastive denoising with rationale rephrasing method. This accuracy is
further enhanced in the voting method, which demonstrates the
effectiveness of the self-consistency approach in improving reasoning
performance.

###### Observation F.2 (Noisy rationales with CD-CoT does not outperform full clean rationales).

The accuracy before voting and the accuracy after voting in CD-CoT do
not exceed the accuracy of the base model prompted with 4 clean
rationales. This indicates that while CD-CoT is effective at denoising
rationales and enhancing reasoning performance in noisy situations, it
does not inherently improve reasoning beyond the capabilities of the
model when clean rationales are provided.

###### Observation F.3 (Contrastive denoising and voting in CD-CoT are important).

The results highlight the importance and effectiveness of the two key
aspects of CD-CoT: (1) contrastive denoising with rationale rephrasing,
which helps to filter out the noise and align the format of the
rationales, and (2) repeated reasoning with voting, which enhances the
robustness and accuracy of the final predictions. Notably, CD-CoT proves
to be a valuable approach for improving the reasoning performance in the
presence of noisy rationales, though it does not surpass the performance
with full clean rationales.

###### Observation F.4 (CD-CoT is more effective at managing irrelevant noise compared to inaccurate noise).

As shown in
Tab. [33](#A6.T33 "Table 33 ‣ F.2 Supplementary Results of the Main Experiments ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
CD-CoT is more effective at handling irrelevant noise than it is at
managing inaccurate noise at the same level. This is because irrelevant
noise is easier to differentiate from the target information, making it
simpler to remove during the rephrasing step of the contrasting
denoising process. Additionally,
Appendix. [F.9](#A6.SS9 "F.9 Rephrased Examples of Different Denoising Methods ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")
presents the denoised results for high-noise scenarios.

Variants of prompt for CD-CoT. We also conduct additional experiments to
test the sensitivity of CD-CoT to prompt designs. Specifically, we
generate several variants of prompts for the proposed CD-CoT method,
including a shorter prompt, the default prompt, and a longer one:

We conduct experiments on NoRa-Math Base-9 to compare these three
prompts. The results in
Tab. [35](#A6.T35 "Table 35 ‣ F.3 The Superior Performance and Denoising Effectiveness ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")
show that the performance of CD-CoT is only marginally influenced by
these prompts. Note that the prompt of CD-CoT remains simple.
Cooperating with advanced methods for iterating prompts and rationales,
such as APE [Zhou et al. \[2022b\]](#bib.bib110) and Star [Zelikman
et al. \[2022\]](#bib.bib97), can further improve this method.

|                           |                   |                   |
|---------------------------|-------------------|-------------------|
| method                    | Irrelevant-medium | Inaccurate-medium |
| Base Model                | 0.284             | 0.082             |
| CD-CoT w/ original prompt | 0.49              | 0.48              |
| CD-CoT w/ short prompt    | 0.46              | 0.46              |
| CD-CoT w/ long prompt     | 0.47              | 0.48              |

Table 35: Ablation study of different prompts in CD-CoT.

Rephrasing performance of SD. Besides, we observe that the SD method
performs better on some tasks when using irrelevant and inaccurate
rationales compared to clean ones. A thorough examination of the
empirical results from the log files reveals some inconsistencies in the
performance of the SD method, especially in the NoRa-Symbolic Equal and
NoRa-Commonsense tasks.

Specifically, for NoRa-Symbolic Equal task:

- •
  In this task, comparing SD and SC reveals that the domain knowledge of
  LLM is relatively weak. According to the experiment results, when SD
  rephrases symbolic tasks, it often directly returns the final answer
  of the original response in many cases.
- •
  The quality of the rephrased results basically does not change with
  the increase in noise.
- •
  As a result, the accuracies of SD under clean and noisy settings are
  very close, with a maximum difference of less than 1%, which we think
  is within the acceptable range of error.

For NoRa-Commonsense task:

- •
  We observe different behaviors of SD in clean and noisy settings.
- •
  In noisy settings, SD does not follow the prompt to make minor
  corrections. Instead, SD generates short rationales with only the
  answer, discarding the original rationale (as shown in
  Tables. [36](#A6.T36 "Table 36 ‣ F.3 The Superior Performance and Denoising Effectiveness ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")-[37](#A6.T37 "Table 37 ‣ F.3 The Superior Performance and Denoising Effectiveness ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")).
  This simplification can benefit commonsense tasks where the model
  already has a strong inherent understanding.
- •
  On the other hand, in the clean setting, SD unexpectedly generates
  entirely new rationales instead of making minor corrections. These new
  rationales may deviate from the original correct reasoning,
  potentially introducing errors or inconsistencies that weren’t present
  in the initial, accurate solution.

In
Tab. [36](#A6.T36 "Table 36 ‣ F.3 The Superior Performance and Denoising Effectiveness ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")
and
Tab. [37](#A6.T37 "Table 37 ‣ F.3 The Superior Performance and Denoising Effectiveness ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
we present two representative examples of SD in the commonsense task.
Our findings show that: (i) when provided with a clear rationale, SD
generates entirely new rationales rather than simply making minor
corrections; (ii) when given a noisy rationale, SD produces brief
rationales that include only the answer, completely discarding the
original rationale.

Overall, SD’s denoising effect relies on LLMs’ intrinsic capability. SD
performs five maskings and reconstructions per noisy example,
concatenating results into five prompts for LLM inference. In contrast,
SC performs five direct inferences on noisy prompts without explicit
denoising. By comparing SD and SC’s performance, we can gain insights
into the model’s denoising effects.

In
Tab. [3](#S3.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 3.2 Tasks and Statistics ‣ 3 The NoRa Dataset ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
SC outperforms SD in NoRa-Math and NoRa-Symbolic tasks, while SD only
marginally excels in the NoRa-Commonsense task. This pattern persists in
the clean settings. This suggests that LLMs struggle to reconstruct
masked prompts in complex, domain-specific tasks but perform better in
simpler common tasks, highlighting the varying levels of internal
knowledge within LLMs across different domains.

Besides, the counterintuitive results on the NoRa-Commonsense task may
be attributed to task-specific characteristics. Observation of
reconstructed masked prompts in this task indicates that LLMs tend to
bypass reconstruction instructions in noisy settings, directly providing
final answers. This accidental removal of all rationales, including
noisy ones, effectively acts as a noise filter. Combined with the LLM’s
natural strength in the commonsens task, this unplanned filtering likely
explains the small improvement in accuracy under noisy conditions.

[TABLE]

[TABLE]

### F.4 The Difficulty of Irrelevant Noise Semantics

We conduct extra experiments with different semantic difficulties.
Specifically, we set up three levels of semantic difficulty:

- •
  Level-1: Topic-irrelevant. Noisy thought has little relevance to the
  task. Here, noisy thought involves concepts that are clearly
  irrelevant to the knowledge required for the task.

  For example, (1) in physics, the number one is the charge of a single
  unit of positive or negative electricity; (2) in chemistry, two is the
  atomic number of helium, the second most abundant element in the
  universe. These two are irrelevant thoughts.
- •
  Level-2: Topic-relevant and task-irrelevant. The noisy thought has
  some apparent relevance to the task but does not help solve the
  problem. Noisy thought may involve some terminology or concepts that
  are relevant to the task but do not provide useful information.

  For example, (1) the modulo operation (mod) finds the remainder after
  the division of one number by another. (2) In base-9, the leading
  digit 1 indicates that the number is greater than or equal to
  $`1*9^{2}=81`$ but less than $`2*9^{2}=162`$. (3) In base-9, the
  number 9 is represented as ’10’ since it is the base value itself. (4)
  The number 11 in base-9 is equivalent to the decimal value
  ninety-nine, which is one less than the square of the base value.
- •
  Level-3: Topic-relevant, task-relevant, but not helpful. Here, the
  noisy thought has some relevance to the task, but is not necessary
  information to solve the problem. The noisy thought may involve
  concepts, principles, or methods that are relevant to the task, but
  not directly applicable to the problem at hand.

  For example, (1) in a base-9 system, the place values are units,
  nines, eighty-ones, and so on, analogous to units, tens, hundreds,
  etc., in base-10; (2) the concept of carrying is essential when
  performing addition in positional numeral systems like base-9 and
  base-10. The modulo operation is a crucial step in the process of
  adding numbers in base-9, as it determines the digit and carry
  values; (3) the modulo operation is a crucial step in the process of
  adding numbers in base-9, as it determines the digit and carry values.

Based on the above definition and prompt, we generate the data with the
following four steps.

- •
  Step-1: Initial generation and human evaluation. We used this prompt
  to generate an initial set of irrelevant statements for each level.
  These were then manually reviewed and filtered to ensure they
  accurately represented the intended level of irrelevance. We selected
  high-quality examples for each level.
- •
  Step-2: Scaled generation. Using these high-quality examples as ICL
  demonstrations, we prompted GPT-4 to generate a larger set of
  irrelevant statements for each level.
- •
  Step-3: Validation through similarity analysis. To confirm that our
  generated statements indeed represented different levels of
  irrelevance, we conducted a cosine similarity analysis. This analysis
  quantitatively demonstrated the semantic differences between levels,
  as shown in our previous response.
- •
  Step-4: Dataset construction. we integrated these generated irrelevant
  statements into our dataset. We inserted them into relevant
  demonstrations at appropriate positions, following a probability
  distribution that corresponds to the intended difficulty level of the
  task.

The experiment results with the above irrelevant thoughts are elaborated
as follows: Here, the setup is kept the same as the main experiments to
be consistent. Therein, we test the GPT-3.5 model with 3-shot irrelevant
rationales where the noise ratio is 0.8.

For a reasonable quantification of relevance, we choose the metric of
cosine similarity with the [embeddings
API](https://platform.openai.com/docs/guides/embeddings) provided by
OpenAI with model: text-embedding-ada-002. We calculate the cosine
similarity between all noise-inserted sample sentences and the original
clean inference sentences and then take their average value.
````
