|----------------|------------------------------------|------------|--------|
| Dataset        | Semantic level                     | Method     | Acc(%) |
| Math Base-9    | Level-1 (Cosine Similarity = 0.75) | base model | 26.6   |
|                |                                    | CD-CoT     | 58.3   |
|                | Level-2 (Cosine Similarity = 0.87) | base model | 30.6   |
|                |                                    | CD-CoT     | 60.0   |
|                | Level-3 (Cosine Similarity = 0.88) | base model | 33.0   |
|                |                                    | CD-CoT     | 59.7   |
| Symbolic Equal | Level-1 (Cosine Similarity = 0.73) | base model | 23.0   |
|                |                                    | CD-CoT     | 44.0   |
|                | Level-2 (Cosine Similarity = 0.79) | base model | 26.9   |

Table 38: Performance of GPT-3.5-turbo under different noise semantics.

For comparison, the accuracy when prompting with clean rationales is
46.4% for NoRa-Math Base-9 and 32.7% for NoRa-Symbolic Equal.
Specifically, we have the following observations:

###### Observation F.5 (CD-CoT method is still effective under different semantic levels of noise).

Notably, we further verify that the proposed CD-CoT method is still
effective under this imperfect scenario with different semantic levels
of noise. Specifically, CD-CoT can achieve 58.3%, 60.0%, and 59.7%
accuracy on the three semantic levels of noise for the NoRa-Math Base-9
tasks and 44.0%, 46.0%, and 45.0% accuracy on the NoRa-Symbolic Equal
tasks. Compared to the base model, CD-CoT shows a significant
improvement of 29.4% and 19.1% in accuracy on semantic level-2 reasoning
for the NoRa-Math Base-9 and NoRa-Symbolic Equal tasks, respectively.
Similarly, CD-CoT demonstrates a 26.7% and 17.3% increase in accuracy on
semantic level-3 reasoning for the NoRa-Math Base-9 and NoRa-Symbolic
Equal tasks.

###### Observation F.6 (CD-CoT maintains a robust and stable performance across different semantic levels of noise.).

As the semantic level of noise increases, the base model exhibits a
certain level of robustness to the irrelevant noise but still suffers a
considerable performance drop (15.8% and 5.8% decrease for semantic
level-2, and 13.4% and 5.0% decrease for semantic level-3 on the
NoRa-Math Base-9 and NoRa-Symbolic Equal tasks, respectively). In
contrast, CD-CoT maintains a robust and stable performance across
different semantic levels of noise.

### F.5 The Number of Noisy Thoughts

As introduced in the main text, we use noise ratios of 0.3, 0.5, and 0.8
to control the number of noisy thoughts. The statistics of the NoRa
dataset are shown in
Tab. [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 3.2 Tasks and Statistics ‣ 3 The NoRa Dataset ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
showing the exact number of clean/noisy thoughts in each task. NoRa-Math
entails 8 thoughts, NoRa-Symbolic varies from 2 to 12, and
NoRa-Commonsense requires 5 thoughts.

To figure out the exact number of noisy thoughts for the model to be
robust, we conduct further experiments to cover a wider range of noisy
thoughts. Specifically,

- •
  We compare the model’s performance on NoRa-Math Base-9 and
  NoRa-Symbolic Equal tasks using clean and noisy rationales containing
  varying numbers of irrelevant or inaccurate thoughts.
- •
  We gradually increase the number of noisy thoughts from 1 to 6 to
  investigate the impact of the noise level on the model’s accuracy.
- •
  The total number of thoughts per shot, including both clean and noisy
  thoughts, is shown in parentheses for the NoRa-Math Base-9 task.
- •
  For the NoRa-Symbolic Equal task, the number of thoughts in the clean
  demonstrations varies across different examples.
- •
  To ensure a consistent comparison, we select a subset of examples with
  a similar number of thoughts (ranging from 8 to 12) for both clean and
  noisy rationale experiments.
- •
  We report the average number of thoughts (10.2) for the clean
  rationales in this subset.
- •
  Consequently, the total number of thoughts per shot for noisy
  rationales in the NoRa-Symbolic Equal task is also calculated based on
  this average.

Table 39: Impact of the number of noisy thoughts on GPT-3.5 performance.

According to the experimental results in the
Tab. [39](#A6.T39 "Table 39 ‣ F.5 The Number of Noisy Thoughts ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
we can observe that:

###### Observation F.7 (The impact of noisy thoughts varies across different noise types).

For the NoRa-Math Base-9 task, the model’s accuracy remains relatively
stable when only one irrelevant noisy thought is introduced. However,
the accuracy drops significantly as the number of irrelevant noisy
thoughts increases. On the other hand, for the NoRa-Symbolic Equal task,
even a single irrelevant or inaccurate noisy thought can lead to a
noticeable decrease in accuracy.

###### Observation F.8 (Inaccurate noisy thoughts have a more severe impact on the model’s performance compared to irrelevant noisy thoughts).

In the NoRa-Math Base-9 task, the accuracy drops drastically from 46.4%
to 34.8% with just one inaccurate, noisy thought, and it further
deteriorates to 6.2% when the number of inaccurate, noisy thoughts
reaches 6. In contrast, with 6 irrelevant noisy thoughts, the accuracy
decreases to 25.3%, which is still higher than that of inaccurate
thoughts.

###### Observation F.9 (Model’s robustness to noisy thoughts varies depending on the nature of the task).

The NoRa-Symbolic Equal task exhibits a similar trend, where the
accuracy decreases as the number of noisy thoughts increases, regardless
of the noise type. However, the decline in accuracy is more gradual
compared to the NoRa-Math Base-9 task, suggesting that the model’s
robustness to noisy thoughts varies depending on the nature of the task.

The experimental results demonstrate that the presence of noisy
thoughts, even in small numbers, can significantly impact the model’s
reasoning performance. The extent of the impact depends on factors such
as the task type, noise type, and number of noisy thoughts.

### F.6 The Number of Noisy Examples

In this part, we provide a deeper analysis and extract more insights
into in-context learning with varying numbers of noisy examples.

First, we obtain the accuracy of the base model with different numbers
of noisy prompting examples, including 0-shot, 1-shot, 3-shot, and
5-shot. According to the results shown in
Tab. [40](#A6.T40 "Table 40 ‣ F.6 The Number of Noisy Examples ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
we propose the following hypothesis: The trend of model performance as
the number of noisy examples increases depends on the change in
performance between 1-shot and 0-shot learning.

Namely, if the performance in 1-shot learning is better than in 0-shot
learning, this indicates that noisy examples still help the model in
recognizing task patterns. Therefore, model performance can improve with
an increasing number of noisy examples, albeit at a slower rate compared
to clean examples. On the other hand, if the performance in one-shot
learning is less than in zero-shot learning, it suggests that noisy
examples interfere with the model’s existing knowledge of the task,
causing model performance to decline as the number of noisy examples
increases.

|         | ina.-hard | 7.2                  | 11.3   | 6.0    | 5.7    |

|         | irr. hard | 5.5                  | 7.7    | 10.7   | 16.1   |

|         | irr. hard | 0.0                  | 1.9    | 6.0    | —      |

Next, we conduct a further quantitative analysis of the impact of noisy
shots on model performance. We evaluate (0-shot, 1-shot, 3-shot) results
partition, there are 8 possible results in total: (CCC), (CCW), (CWC),
(CWW), (WCC), (WCW), (WWC), (WWW). Specifically, ’C’ stands for correct
and ’W’ stands for wrong. For example, CCC means the cases in which test
questions are correctly answered in 0-shot (C), 1-shot (C), and 3-shot
(C). WWC means the cases in which test questions are wrongly answered in
0-shot (W), 1-shot (W), and correctly answered in 3-shot (C).

To analyze the effects of noisy shots, we categorize these eight results
into three types:

- •
  The model answers correctly at zero-shot but starts to answer
  incorrectly as noisy shots increase. The corresponding results are
  (CCW), (CWC), and (CWW).
- •
  The model answers incorrectly at zero-shot but starts to answer
  correctly as noisy shots increase. The corresponding results are
  (WCC), (WCW), and (WWC).
- •
  The model’s performance is consistent at zero-shot and with noisy
  shots. The corresponding results are (CCC) and (WWW).

By observing
Tab. [41](#A6.T41 "Table 41 ‣ F.6 The Number of Noisy Examples ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
we arrive at the following analysis.

###### Observation F.10 (Compared to zero-shot, models can benefit from noisy shots in many cases).

Across all datasets, (WWW) is the most prevalent outcome, while the
second most common results are almost always (WWC) or (WCW). This
implies that there is a relatively large proportion of samples for which
the model answers incorrectly at zero-shot, yet correctly as noisy shots
increase.

###### Observation F.11 (The instances where noisy shots have a negative effect are very rare.).

For the first category of results, namely (CCW), (CWC), and (CWW), the
average proportion across all datasets is 0.039. This indicates that the
proportion of samples where the model answers correctly at zero-shot but
incorrectly as noisy shots increase is very small. In other words, the
instances where noisy shots have a negative effect are rare.

###### Observation F.12 (There are significantly more instances where the model benefits from increasing noisy shots).

For the second category of results, which includes (WCC), (WCW), and
(WWC), the average proportion across all datasets is 0.186, which is
greater than that of the first category. This means that there is a
larger proportion of samples where the model answers incorrectly at
zero-shot but correctly as noisy shots increase. Namely, there are more
instances where noisy shots have a positive effect.

###### Observation F.13 (The type of instances where the model produces consistent answers is dominant across all datasets).

For the third category of results, namely (CCC) and (WWW), the average
proportion across all datasets is the highest, at 0.776. This category
of results does not allow for an assessment of the effect of noisy
shots.

We also provide a case study of in-context learning using a varying
number of noisy examples. We provide cases corresponding to some
possible outcomes (i.e., \[(CCC), (CCW), (CWC)\]) for (0-shot, 1-shot,
3-shot) on the NoRa-Math Base-9 dataset with inaccurate easy-level noise
in

### F.7 Variants of CD-CoT

Acquiring a human-annotated and clean rationale can be challenging in
some practical scenarios. However, we would further clarify the
relatively low cost and the necessity of this clean rationale to
guarantee the effectiveness of robust reasoning.

Current LLMs cannot discern and remove noisy thoughts with their
intrinsic denoising ability, even when enhanced with the self-denoising
method. This is supported by the empirical observations in
Sec. [4](#S4 "4 Evaluating Language Models on NoRa dataset ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").
Hence, external supervision is necessary, which should be sufficient for
denoising and accessible in practice. Existing methods with external
supervision require (1) oracle feedback on the test question, (2) human
feedback of errors on specific tokens or positions, or (3) expert
knowledge to construct detailed descriptions of specific tasks.

By contrast, we believe that a clean CoT demonstration is more
attainable and practical, which can be the minimal requirement for
denoising-purpose prompting. With the proposed CD-CoT method, we show
that LLMs can identify noisy thoughts by contrasting a noisy rationale
with a clean one.

Besides, we build a self-supervised variant of CD-CoT that does not rely
on manually crafted clean examples, i.e., this variant can get a clean
rationale automatically. Specifically, we prompt LLM with a fixed
question using the zero-shot CoT approach [Kojima et al.
\[2022\]](#bib.bib39), allowing it to answer N times. We then select the
responses with the correct answer based on the ground-truth label.

Then, we conduct experiments on two datasets, NoRa-Math Base-9 and
NoRa-Symbolic Equal, with the default model (GPT-3.5-0613). The
hyper-parameters are temperature=0.1 and top-p=1. We set N=50 for
NoRa-Math Base-9 and N=100 for NoRa-Symbolic Equal. Other settings are
kept the same as default with the original CD-CoT.

|         |           |            |                   |        |
|---------|-----------|------------|-------------------|--------|
| Task    | Setting   | \#Method   |                   |        |
|         |           | Base Model | Variant of CD-CoT | CD-CoT |
| Base-9  | irr.-med  | 30.3       | 47.0              | 60.7   |
|         | ina.-med  | 10.1       | 47.0              | 58.7   |
| Sym.(E) | irr.-easy | 28.1       | 33.0              | 44.7   |
|         | ina.-easy | 29.1       | 32.0              | 42.6   |

Table 45: Performances of different variants of CD-CoT.

On the NoRa-Math Base-9 dataset, the LLM answers incorrectly in the
initial 20 attempts. When we increase N to 50, the LLM gets it right
twice. We use one of the self-generated correct rationales to execute
CD-CoT, testing the effect of LLM’s self-generated rationale. On the
NoRa-Symbolic Equal dataset, we need to increase N to 100 to get a
correct self-generated response.

Regarding the empirical results in
Tab. [45](#A6.T45 "Table 45 ‣ F.7 Variants of CD-CoT ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
we derive the following detailed analysis.

###### Observation F.14 (The self-supervised variant of CD-CoT achieves enhancements across various tasks).

On NoRa-Math Base-9, the average accuracy is increased by 26.8
percentage points. On NoRa-Symbolic Equal, it gains an average increase
of 3.9 points in accuracy.

###### Observation F.15 (LLM struggles to generate a clean and correct rationale on its own).

Considering the fact that the model requires 50 attempts on the simple
NoRa-Math Base-9 task to obtain the correct result, it indicates that
the LLM struggles to generate a clean and correct rationale on its own.
Additionally, experiments on the NoRa-Symbolic Equal indicate that as
the task’s difficulty increases, it becomes more challenging for the
model to resolve it by relying solely on its inherent capabilities.

###### Observation F.16 (Self-generated clean examples are not as effective as manually designed clean examples).

Although the self-supervised variant of CD-CoT acquires improvement
across tasks, its gains on the difficult NoRa-Symbolic Equal tasks are
quite modest, with an average increase of only 3.9 points. Conversely,
the original CD-CoT registers an average accuracy enhancement of 39.5
points on NoRa-Math Base-9 tasks and 15.1 points on NoRa-Symbolic Equal
tasks, respectively. We can conclude that self-generated clean examples
can provide some guidance to the LLM on simpler tasks, but they are not
as effective as manually designed clean examples. On more difficult
tasks, the guidance provided by self-generated clean examples is very
limited, and these tasks require additional supervision to steer the
model effectively.

In addition to the self-supervised CD-CoT, exploring strategies like
using contrasting noisy examples or incorporating an external knowledge
base, possibly through a retrieval-augmented denoising framework, may
offer significant advances in automation and robustness of reasoning. We
believe that these are promising directions that deserve further
investigation.

### F.8 New Datasets and Large-scale Real Problems

Recall that the constructed NoRa benchmark covers five prevailing
datasets from three different domains. The current LLMs present
significant vulnerability to noisy rationales in all five datasets of
NoRa, while the proposed method CD-CoT has shown advanced and consistent
robustness against noisy rationales. Specifically,

- •
  Robustness with different datasets: CD-CoT consistently outperforms
  other methods in all five datasets in NoRa.
- •
  Robustness with different noise levels: The results shown in
  Tab. [8](#S5.T8 "Table 8 ‣ 5.2 Empirical Study ‣ 5 Method ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")
  demonstrate the remarkable robustness of CD-CoT to varying noise
  levels. Across the NoRa-Math, NoRa-Symbolic, and NoRa-Commonsense
  tasks, the performance decline of CD-CoT remains modest as the noise
  level increases.
- •
  Robust with different LLMs: The results in
  Tab. [12](#S5.T12 "Table 12 ‣ 5.2 Empirical Study ‣ 5 Method ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")
  further indicate that CD-CoT substantially improves over all three
  other baselines on the more powerful LLMs. Even on the relatively
  smaller Mistral-8x7B, CD-CoT significantly outperforms the other
  baselines on most tasks.

Note that
Sec. [3](#S3 "3 The NoRa Dataset ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")
introduces a general framework for generating noisy rationales with
existing datasets. This means, more datasets can be integrated into NoRa
if necessary in future research. Here, we conduct additional experiments
to generalize CD-CoT to three new datasets that are not covered in NoRa,
including GSM-8K [Cobbe et al. \[2021\]](#bib.bib12),
Blocksworld [Valmeekam et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib82), and BIG-Bench Hard
Dyck Languages [Srivastava et al. \[2022\]](#bib.bib72). Specifically,

- •
  GSM-8K: A math dataset of linguistically diverse grade school math
  word problems.
- •
  Blocksworld: A planning dataset simulating block stacking tasks.
- •
  BIG-Bench Hard Dyck Languages: A symbolic dataset designed for
  predicting the sequence of closing parentheses in a Dyck-4 word.

Then, we generate noisy rationales and compare the following setups:

- •
  Zero-shot: Base model with no demonstration.
- •
  CoT (clean rationales): Base model with 3 clean demonstrations.
- •
  CoT (noisy rationales): Base model with 3 noisy demonstrations.
- •
  CD-CoT (noisy rationales): Base model with 3 noisy demonstrations and
  our CD-CoT method.

The reasoning accuracy in
Tab. [46](#A6.T46 "Table 46 ‣ F.8 New Datasets and Large-scale Real Problems ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?")
shows that CD-CoT consistently outperforms the zero-shot setting and
standard CoT prompting under noisy rationales. This is consistent with
the findings in
Sec. [5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Empirical Study ‣ 5 Method ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
showing CD-CoT’s strong capability of generalization to new datasets.

|  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| Dataset | Setup | \#Method |  |  |  |
|  |  | Zero-shot | CoT (clean) | CoT (noisy) | CD-CoT (noisy) |
| GSM-8K | 300 samples | 84.3 | 87.7 | 84.3 | 86.0 |
| Blocksworld | 200 samples | 2.0 | 25.0 | 13.0 | 25.5 |
| Hard Dyck Languages | 250 samples | 12.4 | 40.8 | 29.2 | 35.2 |

Table 46: Performances of different methods on new datasets.

Along with the introduction of new datasets, we also examine the issue
of noisy rationale in large-scale real-world scenarios, particularly in
conversational question-answering (QA) tasks. While our primary focus is
on reasoning tasks, we discuss the differences and relationships between
these areas.

Conversational QA tasks are usually conducted in a zero-shot setting,
where users directly input the task instructions and hardly provide
examples to the model. NoRa, on the other hand, considers the in-context
learning scenario, where the model is given a few examples before
answering questions. In-context learning can significantly enhance the
model’s performance on complex or new tasks. The purpose of NoRa is to
assess the model’s robustness to noisy examples in in-context learning.

However, for multi-turn conversational QA tasks, the content of previous
answers can be considered as context for the model when answering the
next question. Therefore, by evaluating how noisy context affects a
model’s subsequent question responses, we can explore the issue of noisy
rationales within the realm of conversational QA tasks.

We conduct experiments on MT-Bench [Zheng et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib108)
to investigate the noisy rationale problem in QA scenarios. MT-Bench is
a multi-turn question collection and covers eight principal types of
tasks: Writing, Roleplay, Extraction, Reasoning, Math, Coding, Knowledge
I, and Knowledge II.

For clean context, we directly use the original question to prompt the
model, and the first round of dialogue generated serves as the clean
context. For noisy context, we prompt the model to insert some
irrelevant information as noise when answering the first turn’s
question, and the first round of dialogue serves as the noisy context.
We then continue to use the second turn’s question along with the
generated context to prompt the model to obtain the target response.

We conduct conversational QA tasks on GPT-3.5-turbo-0613 and score the
generated responses with GPT-4. The GPT-4 is to score the responses from
both the first and second turns separately.

|               |                |                 |
|---------------|----------------|-----------------|
| Setting       | \#Avg.score    |                 |
|               | First Response | Second Response |
| Clean Context | 8.73           | 8.23            |
| Noisy Context | 8.16           | 7.75            |

Table 47: Average scores with different contexts.

|  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| Round | Setting | \#Task |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
|  |  | Writing | Roleplay | Extraction | Reasoning | Math | Coding | STEM | Humanities |
| First Response | Clean Context | 9.8 | 8.45 | 9.3 | 6.9 | 8.9 | 7.0 | 9.7 | 9.8 |
|  | Noisy Context | 9.75 | 8.0 | 8.5 | 7.7 | 7.5 | 6.7 | 8.6 | 8.5 |

Table 48: Average scores across types of tasks with different contexts.

As the results shown in
Tab. [47](#A6.T47 "Table 47 ‣ F.8 New Datasets and Large-scale Real Problems ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
the noisy context decreases both the scores (quality) of the first-round
and second-round QA. Specifically, the average scores across different
types of tasks are shown in
Tab. [48](#A6.T48 "Table 48 ‣ F.8 New Datasets and Large-scale Real Problems ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").
Notably, a 0.48% decrease in average score appears in the second
response, which is the final target of the conversation.

[TABLE]

In addition, we provide a case study for illustration. As shown in
Tab. [49](#A6.T49 "Table 49 ‣ F.8 New Datasets and Large-scale Real Problems ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
the noisy context in round 1 leads to a bad, noisy response in round 2.

In the clean context, the model provides an accurate analysis and answer
to the initial question. When the correct round serves as the context
for the second question, the model consistently delivers a precise
analysis and response.

In the noisy context, the model’s response and analysis of the initial
question contain errors. Specifically, as shown in
Tab. [49](#A6.T49 "Table 49 ‣ F.8 New Datasets and Large-scale Real Problems ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
it incorrectly counts multiple integers for both case 1 and case 2,
culminating in a wrong answer for the first question. When the flawed
round serves as the context for the second question, the model generates
the same intermediate results as in the first round and ultimately
provides an incorrect answer to the second question.

In summary, during multi-turn conversational QA tasks, the model’s
response to the current question is influenced by the preceding dialogue
content (i.e., the context), particularly for tasks that require
reasoning and mathematical computations.

|      Self-polish |  To solve 86+57 in base-9, we need to add the digits step by step. In base-9, the digits range from 0 to 8. First, let’s add the rightmost digits, which are 6 (from 86) and 7 (from 57). In base-10, their sum is 13. Since we are in base-9, where the maximum value for a single digit is 8, we need to carry over the excess value. To calculate the carry, we find the remainder when 13 is divided by 9. In this case, the remainder is 4. So, the rightmost digit of the sum is 4, and we carry over 1 to the next column. Next, we add the next digits, which are 8 (from 86) and 5 (from 57), along with the carry of 1. In base-10, their sum is 14. Again, to account for the base-9 system, we find the remainder when 14 is divided by 9. The remainder is 5. Therefore, the next digit of the sum is 5, and we carry over 1 to the next column. Since there are no more digits left to add, we simply include the carry of 1 as the leading digit. Thus, the final result in base-9 is 154. Answer: 154 |

We have 8 + 5 + 1 = 14 in base 10. 14 mod 9 = 5, so the digit is 5 and the carry is 1. A leading digit is 1. So the answer is 154. Answer: 154. |

## Appendix G Further Case Study

In this section, we provide a complete example for NoRa-Math Base-9 of
irrelevant hard-level noise in

present contrastive rephrasing prompts and the resulting rephrased
outcomes for three distinct noisy examples. Following that, the last two
figures exhibit the constructed two diverse contexts along with their

## Appendix H NeurIPS Checklist

1.  1.
    Claims
2.
    Question: Do the main claims made in the abstract and introduction
    accurately reflect the paper’s contributions and scope?
3.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
4.
    Justification: We have summarized the position and key contributions
    of the paper in the abstract and introduction parts.
5.  2.
    Limitations
6.
    Question: Does the paper discuss the limitations of the work
    performed by the authors?
7.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
8.
    Justification: The limitations are discussed in
    Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A Further Discussion ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").
9.  3.
    Theory Assumptions and Proofs
10.
    Question: For each theoretical result, does the paper provide the
    full set of assumptions and a complete (and correct) proof?
11.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
12.
    Justification: The assumptions and proof are provided in
    Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D Theoretical Analysis ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").
13. 4.
    Experimental Result Reproducibility
14.
    Question: Does the paper fully disclose all the information needed
    to reproduce the main experimental results of the paper to the
    extent that it affects the main claims and/or conclusions of the
    paper (regardless of whether the code and data are provided or not)?
15.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
16.
    Justification: The technical details of implementation are
    introduced in
    Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Implementation Details ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").
17. 5.
    Open access to data and code
18.
    Question: Does the paper provide open access to the data and code,
    with sufficient instructions to faithfully reproduce the main
    experimental results, as described in supplemental material?
19.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
20.
    Justification: The source files are publicly available at:
    [https://github.com/tmlr-group/NoisyRationales](https://github.com/tmlr-group/NoisyRationales).
21. 6.
    Experimental Setting/Details
22.
    Question: Does the paper specify all the training and test details
    (e.g., data splits, hyperparameters, how they were chosen, type of
    optimizer, etc.) necessary to understand the results?
23.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
24.
    Justification: The experiment setups are introduced in
    Appendix [F](#A6 "Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?"),
    and technical details of implementation are introduced in
    Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Implementation Details ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").
25. 7.
    Experiment Statistical Significance
26.
    Question: Does the paper report error bars suitably and correctly
    defined or other appropriate information about the statistical
    significance of the experiments?
27.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
28.
    Justification: We conduct evaluations on 300 questions for each task
    and repeat the reasoning 5 times for each question to obtain
    consistent and reliable results. The empirical results with mean and
    std values are provided in
    Tab. [33](#A6.T33 "Table 33 ‣ F.2 Supplementary Results of the Main Experiments ‣ Appendix F Full Experiments ‣ Can Language Models Perform Robust Reasoning in Chain-of-thought Prompting with Noisy Rationales?").
29. 8.
    Experiments Compute Resources
30.
    Question: For each experiment, does the paper provide sufficient
    information on the computer resources (type of compute workers,
    memory, time of execution) needed to reproduce the experiments?
31.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
32.
    Justification: We employ GPT-3.5-turbo-0613 \[[Floridi and
    Chiriatti, 2020](#bib.bib17)\] as our base LLM for the analyses
    presented in this study. In addition, we conduct evaluations on
    three supplementary models, including Gemini-Pro \[[Team et al.,
    2023](#bib.bib76)\], Llama2-70B \[[Touvron et al.,
    2023](#bib.bib79)\], and Mixtral-8x7B \[[Jiang et al.,
    2024](#bib.bib33)\].
33. 9.
    Code Of Ethics
34.
    Question: Does the research conducted in the paper conform, in every
    respect, with the NeurIPS Code of Ethics
    [https://neurips.cc/public/EthicsGuidelines](https://neurips.cc/public/EthicsGuidelines)?
35.
    Answer: \[Yes\]
36.
    Justification: We have carefully checked the NeurIPS Code of Ethics
